I can't shake the nagging feeling that I've missed something, which makes me feel completely chained to my desk. It feels virtually impossible to move on or leave until I actually perform the checks.\n\nDoctor: Have you started avoiding certain tasks or situations at work or in your daily life because of the fear of making a mistake that could lead to severe consequences?\n\nPatient: I don't actually avoid my work tasks, but rather I get obsessively stuck doing them over and over because I'm so terrified of making a catastrophic mistake. However, I have started skipping my personal life, like workouts, paddleboarding meetups, and a beach clean-up I organized, because I feel completely chained to my desk rechecking my work.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your repetitive behaviors of checking and rechecking are aimed at preventing a specific, dreaded event, such as the catastrophic failures you mentioned, or are they more about following a set of rules or achieving a sense of completeness or perfection?\n\nPatient: My checking is definitely driven by the fear of a specific catastrophic event rather than just following rules. I get trapped in these loops because I'm terrified that a tiny error, like a mislabeled bolt or incorrect tolerance, will cause a part to fail and hurt someone. Even though I know it's irrational and also struggle with a nagging feeling of incompleteness, the core driver is preventing those disastrous consequences.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if these catastrophic events you fear, such as parts failing and people getting hurt, are specifically related to your area of work or expertise, or if they are more generalized and could happen in any situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, these fears are specifically tied to my engineering work rather than being general worries. I get looping thoughts about very specific technical details in my CAD designs, like a mislabeled bolt size or a tolerance being off by a fraction, leading to mechanical parts failing and people getting hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these intrusive thoughts and images of catastrophic failures outside of work as well, or are they primarily triggered by your work-related tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: While the thoughts are strictly about my work responsibilities—like design flaws and parts failing—they definitely don't stop when I leave the office. They constantly loop in my head even when I'm at home, which has really started to ruin my sleep and keep me from enjoying my paddleboarding club.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes of major depression, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or a lack of interest in activities you once enjoyed, alongside these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?"

However, I have started skipping my personal life, like workouts, paddleboarding meetups, and a beach clean-up I organized, because I feel completely chained to my desk rechecking my work.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your repetitive behaviors of checking and rechecking are aimed at preventing a specific, dreaded event, such as the catastrophic failures you mentioned, or are they more about following a set of rules or achieving a sense of completeness or perfection?\n\nPatient: My checking is definitely driven by the fear of a specific catastrophic event rather than just following rules. I get trapped in these loops because I'm terrified that a tiny error, like a mislabeled bolt or incorrect tolerance, will cause a part to fail and hurt someone. Even though I know it's irrational and also struggle with a nagging feeling of incompleteness, the core driver is preventing those disastrous consequences.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if these catastrophic events you fear, such as parts failing and people getting hurt, are specifically related to your area of work or expertise, or if they are more generalized and could happen in any situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, these fears are specifically tied to my engineering work rather than being general worries. I get looping thoughts about very specific technical details in my CAD designs, like a mislabeled bolt size or a tolerance being off by a fraction, leading to mechanical parts failing and people getting hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these intrusive thoughts and images of catastrophic failures outside of work as well, or are they primarily triggered by your work-related tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: While the thoughts are strictly about my work responsibilities—like design flaws and parts failing—they definitely don't stop when I leave the office. They constantly loop in my head even when I'm at home, which has really started to ruin my sleep and keep me from enjoying my paddleboarding club.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes of major depression, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or a lack of interest in activities you once enjoyed, alongside these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced episodes of persistent sadness or hopelessness. My missing workouts and paddleboarding isn't because I've lost interest in them, but rather because I feel so chained to my checking routines that I simply run out of time.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific situations or stimuli that trigger these intrusive thoughts and compulsive checking behaviors, or if they seem to occur randomly throughout your day?\n\nPatient: They aren't random at all, but are highly triggered by specific tasks at my desk. Whenever I open a CAD file, draft an email, or review design details like bolt sizes and tolerances, it sets off the thoughts of a failure and the immediate urge to repeatedly recheck."

Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I keep getting stuck redoing the same checks and routines over and over at work, even when I know they’re unnecessary, and it’s starting to derail my projects and my life. It has gotten to the point where I just can't seem to stop myself, and I really need some help to figure out why this is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience repetitive thoughts or urges that are disturbing or intrusive, in addition to these compulsive behaviors of redoing checks and routines?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get these looping, irrational thoughts that if I've missed even a tiny detail, like a mislabeled bolt size, something catastrophic will happen. I keep getting these vivid, disturbing mental images of parts failing and people getting hurt because of my oversight, and I just can't seem to shut them out.\n\nDoctor: Do these repetitive thoughts or urges to redo checks and routines primarily focus on symmetry, exactness, or avoiding harm to yourself or others, or are they more about contamination, losing control, or making mistakes that lead to severe consequences?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely about making a mistake that could lead to severe consequences, rather than symmetry or contamination. I’m consumed by this intense fear that a tiny oversight on my part will cause a catastrophic failure where people actually get hurt. It is the dread of causing that kind of harm through an error that completely drives my checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any significant anxiety or distress when you're prevented from performing these checking rituals or when you try to ignore these intrusive thoughts about potential catastrophic failures?\n\nPatient: Yes, trying to ignore the thoughts causes intense anxiety because those images of parts failing and people getting hurt just loop uncontrollably in my head. I can't shake the nagging feeling that I've missed something, which makes me feel completely chained to my desk. It feels virtually impossible to move on or leave until I actually perform the checks.\n\nDoctor: Have you started avoiding certain tasks or situations at work or in your daily life because of the fear of making a mistake that could lead to severe consequences?\n\nPatient: I don't actually avoid my work tasks, but rather I get obsessively stuck doing them over and over because I'm so terrified of making a catastrophic mistake. However, I have started skipping my personal life, like workouts, paddleboarding meetups, and a beach clean-up I organized, because I feel completely chained to my desk rechecking my work.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your repetitive behaviors of checking and rechecking are aimed at preventing a specific, dreaded event, such as the catastrophic failures you mentioned, or are they more about following a set of rules or achieving a sense of completeness or perfection?\n\nPatient: My checking is definitely driven by the fear of a specific catastrophic event rather than just following rules. I get trapped in these loops because I'm terrified that a tiny error, like a mislabeled bolt or incorrect tolerance, will cause a part to fail and hurt someone. Even though I know it's irrational and also struggle with a nagging feeling of incompleteness, the core driver is preventing those disastrous consequences.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if these catastrophic events you fear, such as parts failing and people getting hurt, are specifically related to your area of work or expertise, or if they are more generalized and could happen in any situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, these fears are specifically tied to my engineering work rather than being general worries. I get looping thoughts about very specific technical details in my CAD designs, like a mislabeled bolt size or a tolerance being off by a fraction, leading to mechanical parts failing and people getting hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these intrusive thoughts and images of catastrophic failures outside of work as well, or are they primarily triggered by your work-related tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: While the thoughts are strictly about my work responsibilities—like design flaws and parts failing—they definitely don't stop when I leave the office. They constantly loop in my head even when I'm at home, which has really started to ruin my sleep and keep me from enjoying my paddleboarding club.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes of major depression, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or a lack of interest in activities you once enjoyed, alongside these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced episodes of persistent sadness or hopelessness. My missing workouts and paddleboarding isn't because I've lost interest in them, but rather because I feel so chained to my checking routines that I simply run out of time.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific situations or stimuli that trigger these intrusive thoughts and compulsive checking behaviors, or if they seem to occur randomly throughout your day?\n\nPatient: They aren't random at all, but are highly triggered by specific tasks at my desk. Whenever I open a CAD file, draft an email, or review design details like bolt sizes and tolerances, it sets off the thoughts of a failure and the immediate urge to repeatedly recheck.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any situations or tasks outside of your work where you also exhibit similar compulsive checking behaviors or feel the urge to do so, or are these behaviors strictly confined to your work-related activities?\n\nPatient: The checking behaviors themselves are strictly confined to my work tasks, like reviewing CAD files, emails, and project plans. However, because these routines consume so much of my time, they severely impact my life outside of work. I've had to miss out on sleep, workouts, and paddleboarding meetups just because I'm stuck at my desk so late."

However, I have started skipping my personal life, like workouts, paddleboarding meetups, and a beach clean-up I organized, because I feel completely chained to my desk rechecking my work.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your repetitive behaviors of checking and rechecking are aimed at preventing a specific, dreaded event, such as the catastrophic failures you mentioned, or are they more about following a set of rules or achieving a sense of completeness or perfection?\n\nPatient: My checking is definitely driven by the fear of a specific catastrophic event rather than just following rules. I get trapped in these loops because I'm terrified that a tiny error, like a mislabeled bolt or incorrect tolerance, will cause a part to fail and hurt someone. Even though I know it's irrational and also struggle with a nagging feeling of incompleteness, the core driver is preventing those disastrous consequences.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if these catastrophic events you fear, such as parts failing and people getting hurt, are specifically related to your area of work or expertise, or if they are more generalized and could happen in any situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, these fears are specifically tied to my engineering work rather than being general worries. I get looping thoughts about very specific technical details in my CAD designs, like a mislabeled bolt size or a tolerance being off by a fraction, leading to mechanical parts failing and people getting hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these intrusive thoughts and images of catastrophic failures outside of work as well, or are they primarily triggered by your work-related tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: While the thoughts are strictly about my work responsibilities—like design flaws and parts failing—they definitely don't stop when I leave the office. They constantly loop in my head even when I'm at home, which has really started to ruin my sleep and keep me from enjoying my paddleboarding club.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes of major depression, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or a lack of interest in activities you once enjoyed, alongside these obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced episodes of persistent sadness or hopelessness. My missing workouts and paddleboarding isn't because I've lost interest in them, but rather because I feel so chained to my checking routines that I simply run out of time.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific situations or stimuli that trigger these intrusive thoughts and compulsive checking behaviors, or if they seem to occur randomly throughout your day?\n\nPatient: They aren't random at all, but are highly triggered by specific tasks at my desk. Whenever I open a CAD file, draft an email, or review design details like bolt sizes and tolerances, it sets off the thoughts of a failure and the immediate urge to repeatedly recheck.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any situations or tasks outside of your work where you also exhibit similar compulsive checking behaviors or feel the urge to do so, or are these behaviors strictly confined to your work-related activities?\n\nPatient: The checking behaviors themselves are strictly confined to my work tasks, like reviewing CAD files, emails, and project plans. However, because these routines consume so much of my time, they severely impact my life outside of work. I've had to miss out on sleep, workouts, and paddleboarding meetups just because I'm stuck at my desk so late.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if your obsessive thoughts about catastrophic failures, such as parts failing and people getting hurt, are exclusively focused on preventing harm to others, or if they also involve fears of harm coming to yourself?\n\nPatient: The fears are almost exclusively focused on others, specifically the end-users who will eventually use the equipment and products I design. I don't really worry about harm coming to myself; it's the mental images of parts failing and causing catastrophic accidents that injure other people that I can't seem to shut out."
  }

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don't actually avoid my work tasks, but rather I get obsessively stuck doing them over and over because I'm so terrified of making a catastrophic mistake. However, I have started skipping my personal life, like workouts, paddleboarding meetups, and a beach clean-up I organized, because I feel completely chained to my desk rechecking my work.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.
